In the [2020 Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_Census), 45,904 New Mexico residents were identified as African American (2.2% of the total).[^cite_note-150] African Americans in the five counties of [Bernalillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernalillo_County,_New_Mexico) (21,344), [Doña Ana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Do%C3%B1a_Ana_County,_New_Mexico) (4,072), [Sandoval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandoval_County,_New_Mexico) (3,327), [Curry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curry_County,_New_Mexico) (2,900), and [Lea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lea_County,_New_Mexico) (2,801) make up more than 75% of all African Americans in the state.[^cite_note-151]

According to the 2022 [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey),[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] the most commonly claimed ancestry groups in New Mexico were:

- [Mexican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_American) (32.8%)

- Other Hispanic ([Hispano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanos_of_New_Mexico)/[Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Americans)) (15.3%)

- [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Americans) (8.0%)

- [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Americans) (7.9%)

- [Irish](775859.md) (6.4%)

- [Diné](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo) (6.3%)

- [Pueblo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pueblo_peoples) (2.4%)

According to 2020 census data, 19.9% of the population identifies as multiracial/mixed-race, a population larger than the Native American, Black, Asian and NHPI population groups.[^cite_note-auto-155] Almost 90% of the multiracial population in New Mexico identifies as Hispanic or Latino.[^cite_note-162]

Birth data

The majority of live births in New Mexico are to Hispanic whites, with Hispanics of any race consistently accounting for over half of all live births since 2013.

### Immigration

A little over 9% of New Mexican residents are foreign-born, and an additional 6.0% of U.S.-born residents live with at least one immigrant parent.[^cite_note-\:1-174] The proportion of foreign-born residents is below the national average of 13.7%, and New Mexico was the only state to see a decline in its immigrant population between 2012 and 2022.[^cite_note-175]

In 2018, the top countries of origin for New Mexico's immigrants were Mexico, the [Philippines](23440.md), India, Germany and [Cuba](5042481.md).[^cite_note-176] As of 2021, the vast majority of immigrants in the state came from Mexico (67.6%), followed by the Philippines (3.1%) and Germany (2.4%).[^cite_note-\:1-174]

Notwithstanding their relatively small population, immigrants play a disproportionately large role in New Mexico's economy, accounting for almost one-eighth (12.5%) of the labor force,15% of entrepreneurs, and 19.1% of personal care aides, as well as 9.1% of workers in STEM fields.[^cite_note-\:1-174]

### Languages

| English only | 64% |
| --- | --- |
| Spanish | 28% |
| [Navajo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo_language) | 4% |
| Others | 4% |

New Mexico ranks third after California and Texas in the number of multilingual residents.[^cite_note-177] According to the [2010 U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_U.S._census), 28.5% of the population age 5 and older speak Spanish at home, while 3.5% speak [Navajo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo_language).[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] Most speakers of [New Mexican Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexican_Spanish) are descendants of pre-18th century Spanish [settlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settler).[^cite_note-180] Contrary to popular belief, New Mexican Spanish is not an archaic form of 17th-century [Castilian Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castilian_Spanish); though some archaic elements exist, linguistic research has determined that the dialect "is neither more Iberian nor more archaic" than other varieties spoken in the Americas.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] Nevertheless, centuries of isolation during the colonial period insulated the New Mexican dialect from "standard" Spanish, leading to the preservation of older vocabulary as well as its own innovations.[^cite_note-Atlas_retentions-183][^cite_note-Atlas_innovations-184]

Besides Navajo, which is also spoken in Arizona, several other [Native American languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_languages_of_the_Americas) are spoken by smaller groups in New Mexico, most of which are endemic to the state. Native New Mexican languages include [Mescalero Apache](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mescalero-Chiricahua_language), [Jicarilla Apache](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jicarilla_language), [Tewa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tewa_language), [Southern Tiwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Tiwa_language), [Northern Tiwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Tiwa), [Towa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jemez_language), [Keres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keres_language) (Eastern and Western), and [Zuni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zuni_language). Mescalero and Jicarilla Apache are closely related [Southern Athabaskan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Athabaskan_languages), and both are also related to Navajo. Tewa, the Tiwa languages, and Towa belong to the [Kiowa-Tanoan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanoan_languages) language family, and thus all descend from a [common ancestor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_family). Keres and Zuni are [language isolates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_isolate) with no relatives outside of New Mexico.

#### Official language

New Mexico's original state constitution of 1911 required all laws be published in both English and Spanish for twenty years after ratification;[^cite_note-185] this requirement was renewed in 1931 and 1943,[^cite_note-Cobarrubia-186] with some sources stating the state was officially bilingual until 1953.[^cite_note-187] Nonetheless, the current constitution does not declare any language "official".[^cite_note-const-188] While Spanish was permitted in the legislature until 1935, all state officials are required to have good knowledge of English; consequently, some analysts argue that New Mexico cannot be considered a bilingual state, since not all laws are published in both languages.[^cite_note-Cobarrubia-186]

The state legislature remains constitutionally empowered to publish laws in English and Spanish and to appropriate funds for translation. Whenever a [referendum](51582.md) to approve an amendment to the New Mexican constitution is held, ballots must be printed in both English and Spanish.[^cite_note-189] Certain legal notices must be published in both English and Spanish, and the state maintains a list of newspapers for Spanish publication.[^cite_note-190]

In the judiciary, witnesses and defendants have the right to testify in either language, and monolingual speakers of Spanish have the same right to serve on juries as English speakers.[^cite_note-const-188][^cite_note-191] In public education, the state is constitutionally obligated to provide bilingual education and Spanish-speaking instructors in school districts where the majority of students are [Hispanophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanophone).[^cite_note-const-188] The constitution also provides that citizens who speak neither English nor Spanish have a right to vote, hold public office, and serve on juries.[^cite_note-192]

In 1989, New Mexico became the first of only four states to officially adopt the [English Plus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Plus) resolution, which supports acceptance of non-English languages.[^cite_note-JosephJE-193] In 1995, the state adopted an official bilingual song, "[New Mexico – Mi Lindo Nuevo México](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_%E2%80%93_Mi_Lindo_Nuevo_M%C3%A9xico)".[^cite_note-Blue_Book_State_Symbols-194]<sup>: 75, 81 </sup> In 2008, New Mexico was the first state to officially adopt a [Navajo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo_language) textbook for use in public schools.[^cite_note-195]

### Religion

See also: [The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints_in_New_Mexico)

Religious self-identification, per [Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute)'s 2025 *American Values Survey*

[^cite_note-\:0-196]

1. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_the_United_States) (34.0%)

2. [Catholicism](410868.md) (25.0%)

3. [Mormonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormonism) (1.00%)

4. [Unaffiliated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_the_United_States) (33.0%)

5. [Judaism](15624.md) (1.00%)

6. Other (6.00%)

Like most U.S. states, New Mexico is predominantly [Christian](18337522.md). According to [Association of Religion Data Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives) (ARDA), the largest denominations in 2010 were the [Catholic Church](606848.md) (684,941 members); the [Southern Baptist Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Baptist_Convention) (113,452); [The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints) (67,637), and the [United Methodist Church](32304.md) (36,424).\[^cite_note-[www.thearda.com-197](http://www.thearda.com-197/)] Approximately one-fifth of residents are [unaffiliated with any religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion), which includes atheists, agnostics, and deists. A 2020 study by the [Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute) (PRRI) determined 67% of the population were Christian, with Roman Catholics constituting the largest denominational group.[^cite_note-198] In 2025, the PRRI estimated 62% of the population were Christian, with Protestants now having become the largest denominational group.[^cite_note-199]



[San Miguel Chapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Miguel_Chapel), built in 1610 in [Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico), is the oldest church structure in the continental U.S.

Roman Catholicism is deeply rooted in New Mexico's history and culture, going back to its settlement by the Spanish in the early 17th century. The oldest Christian church in the continental U.S., and the third oldest in any U.S. state or territory, is the [San Miguel Mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Miguel_Mission) in Santa Fe, which was built in 1610. Within the hierarchy of the Catholic Church, New Mexico belongs to the [ecclesiastical province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiastical_province) of Santa Fe. The state has three [ecclesiastical districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese):[^cite_note-200] the [Archdiocese of Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Santa_Fe), the [Diocese of Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese_of_Gallup), and the [Diocese of Las Cruces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese_of_Las_Cruces).[^cite_note-Frommer's-2023-201] [Evangelicalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism) and [nondenominational Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondenominational_Christianity) have seen growth in the state since the late 20th century: The [Billy Graham Evangelistic Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Graham_Evangelistic_Association) has hosted numerous events in New Mexico,[^cite_note-Billy_Graham_Evangelistic_Association_2021-202][^cite_note-Poling_2007_p._146-203] and Albuquerque has several [megachurches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megachurch), which have numerous satellite locations in the state, including Calvary of Albuquerque, Legacy Church, and Sagebrush Church.[^cite_note-Chief_2021-204]

New Mexico has been a leading center of the [New Age movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Age) since at least the 1960s, attracting adherents from across the country.[^cite_note-Frommer's-2023-201] The state's "thriving New Age network" encompasses various schools of [alternative medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_medicine), [Holistic Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holistic_Health), [psychic healing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_medicine), and [new religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_religious_movement), as well as festivals, pilgrimage sites, spiritual retreats, and communes.[^cite_note-Shukman-2006-205][^cite_note-206] New Mexico's [Japanese American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_American) community has influenced the state's religious heritage, with [Shinto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinto) and [Zen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zen) represented by [Kagyu Shenpen Kunchab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kagyu_Shenpen_Kunchab), [Kōbun Chino Otogawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dbun_Chino_Otogawa), [Upaya Institute and Zen Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upaya_Institute_and_Zen_Center).[^cite_note-Nealson_2001_p._89-207] Likewise, [Holism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holism) is represented in New Mexico, as are associated faiths such as [Buddhism](3267529.md) and [Seventh-day Adventism](28632.md);[^cite_note-Feith_2014_pp._159–170-208][^cite_note-Vasquez_1996_p.-209] a [Tibetan Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Buddhism) temple is located at [Zuni Mountain Stupa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zuni_Mountain_Stupa) in [Grants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grants,_New_Mexico).

Religious education, art, [broadcasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_broadcasting), [media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faith-based_organization) exist across religions and faiths in New Mexico, including [KHAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KHAC), [KXXQ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KXXQ), [Dar al-Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dar_al-Islam_\(organization\)), and *[Intermountain Jewish News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermountain_Jewish_News)*. Christian schools in New Mexico are encouraged to receive [educational accreditation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Educational_accreditation), and among them are the [University of the Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Southwest), [St. Pius High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Pius_X_High_School_\(Albuquerque\)), [Hope Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hope_Christian_School), [Sandia View Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandia_View_Academy), [St. Michael's High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Michael's_High_School), [Las Cruces Catholic School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Cruces_Catholic_School), [St. Bonaventure Indian School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Bonaventure_Indian_School), and [Rehoboth Christian School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rehoboth_Christian_School). [Albuquerque's growing media sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_in_Albuquerque,_New_Mexico) has made it a popular hub for several national [Christian media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_media) institutions, such as [Trinity Broadcasting Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Broadcasting_Network)'s [KNAT-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KNAT-TV). [Christian artistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_art) expression includes the [gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_music) tradition within [New Mexico music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_music),[^cite_note-NPR_Spiritual_Exitos-210] and [contemporary Christian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_Christian_music) such as [KLYT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KLYT) radio station.[^cite_note-Maliskas_2020-211] Several indigenous and Christian religious sites are registered and protected as part of regional and global cultural heritage.[^cite_note-New_Mexico_Tourism_Department-212][^cite_note-New_Mexico_Native_American_Communities-213]

Reflecting centuries of successive migrations and settlements, New Mexico has developed a distinct [syncretic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism) [folk religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_religion) that is centered on [Puebloan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puebloans) traditions and [Hispano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanos_of_New_Mexico) [folk Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_Catholicism), with some elements of [Diné Bahaneʼ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Din%C3%A9_Bahane%CA%BC), [Apache](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apache), [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant), and [Evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism) faiths.[^cite_note-University_of_Arizona_Press_2018-214] This unique religious tradition is sometimes referred to as "Pueblo Christianity" or "Placita Christianity", referring to both the [Pueblos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pueblo) and Hispanic [town squares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town_square).[^cite_note-Hendrickson_2017_p._20-215] Customs and practices include the maintenance of [acequias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acequia),[^cite_note-Czar_2013-216] [Pueblo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pueblo_architecture) and [Territorial Style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_Style) churches,[^cite_note-Czar_2013-216] ceremonial dances such as the *[matachines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matachines),*[^cite_note-Interns_2019-217][^cite_note-The_Wisdom_Archive_2022-218] [religious artistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_art) expression of [kachinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kachina) and [santos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santo_\(art\)),[^cite_note-Mills_Taylor_Museum_p.-219] religious holidays celebrating [saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint) such as Pueblo Feast Days,[^cite_note-Visit_Albuquerque_2017-220] Christmas traditions of [bizcochitos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bizcochito) and [farolitos or luminarias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luminaria),[^cite_note-Gomez_2020-221][^cite_note-NPS.gov_Homepage_(U.S._National_Park_Service)_2021-222] and pilgrimages like that of [El Santuario de Chimayo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Santuario_de_Chimayo).[^cite_note-Trust_for_Public_Land_2022-223] The luminaria tradition is a cultural hallmark of the [Pueblos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puebloans) and [Hispanos of New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanos_of_New_Mexico) and a part of the state's distinct heritage. The luminaria custom has spread [nationwide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luminaria#Elsewhere), both as a Christmas tradition as well as for other events. New Mexico's distinctive faith tradition is believed to reflect the [religious naturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_naturalism) of the state's indigenous and Hispano peoples, who constitute a pseudo [ethnoreligious group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnoreligious_group).[^cite_note-Lamadrid_1993-224]

New Mexico's leadership within otherwise disparate traditions such as Christianity, the [Native American Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_Church), and New Age movements has been linked to its remote and ancient indigenous spirituality, which emphasized sacred connections to nature, and its over 300 years of syncretized Pueblo and Hispano religious and folk customs.[^cite_note-Frommer's-2023-201][^cite_note-Shukman-2006-205] The state's remoteness has likewise been cited as attracting and fostering communities seeking the freedom to practice or cultivate new beliefs.[^cite_note-Shukman-2006-205] Global spiritual leaders including [Billy Graham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Graham) and [Dalai Lama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalai_Lama), along with community leaders of [Hispanic and Latino Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) and [indigenous peoples of the North American Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_North_American_Southwest), have remarked on New Mexico being a [sacred space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_space).[^cite_note-Batchelder_2021-225][^cite_note-Preston_2014-226][^cite_note-Rockman_Steele_2003_p.-227]

According to a 2017 survey by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), New Mexico ranks 18th among the 50 U.S. states in [religiosity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religiosity), 63% of respondents stating they believe in God with certainty, with an additional 20% being fairly certain of the existence of God, while 59% considering religion to be important in their lives and another 20% believe it to be somewhat important.[^cite_note-228] Among its population in 2022, 31% were unaffiliated.[^cite_note-\:0-196]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_New_Mexico)



New Mexico [state quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/50_state_quarters), circulated in April 2008

Oil and gas production, the entertainment industry, [high tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_technology) scientific research, tourism, and government spending are important drivers of the state economy.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-Sector_2014-230][^cite_note-Vickers_2012-231][^cite_note-Forbes_2019-232][^cite_note-233] The state government has an elaborate system of tax credits and technical assistance to promote job growth and business investment, especially in new technologies.[^cite_note-ReferenceB-234] In 2025, 99.0% of businesses in the state were [small businesses](276495.md) and employed 53.3% of its work force.[^cite_note-235]

In 2025, New Mexico's gross domestic product was \$152.778 billion[^cite_note-apps.bea.gov-236] compared to roughly \$80 billion in 2010.[^cite_note-237] State GDP peaked in 2019 at nearly \$99 billion but declined in the face of the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md). New Mexico's [per capita personal income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita_income) was \$61,645 in 2025[^cite_note-apps.bea.gov-236] compared to \$31,474 in 2007;[^cite_note-238] it was the third lowest in the country after West Virginia and Mississippi.[^cite_note-239] The percentage of persons below the [poverty level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_level) has largely plateaued in the 21st century, from 18.4% in 2005 to 18.2% in 2021.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-PopEstUS-118] As of May 2025, the state's unemployment rate was 4.2%.[^cite_note-241]

Traditionally dependent on resource extraction, ranching, and railroad transportation, New Mexico has increasingly shifted towards services, high-end manufacturing, and tourism.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] Since 2017, the state has seen a steady rise in the number of annual visitors, peaking in 2021 at 39.2 million tourists, who had an economic impact of \$10 billion.[^cite_note-AP-2022-244] New Mexico has also seen greater investment in media and scientific research.

## Transportation



In this photo, the [Mexico–United States border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border) divides [Sunland Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunland_Park,_New_Mexico) and the Mexican state of [Chihuahua](23962301.md).

New Mexico has long been an important corridor for trade and [migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_migration). The builders of the ruins at [Chaco Canyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaco_Canyon) also created a radiating network of roads from the mysterious settlement.[^cite_note-245] Chaco Canyon's trade function shifted to [Casas Grandes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casas_Grandes) in the present-day Mexican [state of Chihuahua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Chihuahua); however, north–south trade continued. Ancient Indigenous trade with [Mesoamerican cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesoamerican_cultures) included northbound exotic birds, seashells, and copper. Turquoise, pottery, and salt were some of the goods transported south along the [Rio Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Grande). Present-day New Mexico's pre-Columbian trade is especially remarkable for being undertaken on foot. The north–south trade route later became a path for horse-drawn colonists arriving from [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain) as well as trade and communication; later called *[El Camino Real de Tierra Adentro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Camino_Real_de_Tierra_Adentro),* it was among the four "royal roads" that were crucial lifelines to Spanish colonial possessions in North America.[^cite_note-Suina-246]



Santa Fe trail sign

The [Santa Fe Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_Trail) was the 19th-century territory's vital commercial and military highway link to the Eastern United States.[^cite_note-247] Several trails that terminated in northern New Mexico, including the Camino Real, the Santa Fe Trail and the [Old Spanish Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Spanish_Trail_\(trade_route\)) are recognized as [National Historic Trails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Historic_Trail). New Mexico's latitude and low passes made it an attractive east–west transportation corridor.[^cite_note-248] As a territory, the [Gadsden Purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadsden_Purchase) increased New Mexico's land area for the purpose of constructing a southern [transcontinental railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcontinental_railroad), that of the [Southern Pacific Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Pacific_Railroad). Another transcontinental railroad was completed by the [Atchison, Topeka and Santa Fe Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atchison,_Topeka_and_Santa_Fe_Railway). The railroads essentially replaced the earlier trails but prompted a population boom. Early transcontinental [auto trails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auto_trail) later crossed the state, bringing more migrants. Railroads were later supplemented or replaced by a system of highways and airports. Today, New Mexico's [Interstate Highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_Highway) approximate the earlier land routes of the Camino Real, the Santa Fe Trail, and the transcontinental railroads.

### Road

See also: [Speed limits in the United States by jurisdiction § New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speed_limits_in_the_United_States_by_jurisdiction#New_Mexico), and [List of New Mexico highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_New_Mexico_highways)



Personal automobiles remain the primary means of transportation for most New Mexicans, especially in rural areas.[^cite_note-abqjournal.com-110] The state had 59,927 route miles of highway as of 2000, of which 7,037 receive federal aid.[^cite_note-249] That year there were 1,003 miles (1,614 km) of freeways, of which 1,000 were the route miles of Interstate Highways [10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_10_in_New_Mexico), [25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_25_in_New_Mexico) and [40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_40_in_New_Mexico).[^cite_note-250] The former number has increased with the upgrading of roads near [Pojoaque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pojoaque,_New_Mexico), [Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico), and [Las Cruces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Cruces,_New_Mexico) to freeways. Notable bridges include the [Rio Grande Gorge Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Grande_Gorge_Bridge) near [Taos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taos,_New_Mexico). Larger cities in New Mexico typically have some form of public transportation by road; [ABQ RIDE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABQ_RIDE) is the state's largest such system.[^cite_note-251] Rural and intercity public transportation by road is provided by [Americanos USA, LLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americanos_USA,_LLC), [Greyhound Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greyhound_Lines), and several government operators.

New Mexico is plagued by poor road conditions, with roughly a third of its roadways suffering from "inadequate state and local funding".[^cite_note-By-252] As of 2001, 703 highway bridges, or one percent, were declared "structurally deficient" or "structurally obsolete".[^cite_note-253] Data from 2019 found 207 bridges and more than 3,822 miles of highway in less than subpar condition, resulting in greater commute times and higher costs in vehicles maintenance.[^cite_note-254]

New Mexico has historically had a problem with drunk driving, though this has lessened: According to the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)*, the state once had the nation's highest alcohol-related crash rates but ranked 25th in this regard by 2009.[^cite_note-255] The highway traffic fatality rate was 1.9 per million miles traveled in 2000, the 13th-highest among U.S. states.[^cite_note-256] A 2022 report cited poor road as a major factor in New Mexico's continually high traffic fatalities; between 2015 and 2019, close 1,900 people were killed in automotive crashes in the state.[^cite_note-By-252]

### Highways

See also: [List of U.S. Routes in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._Routes_in_New_Mexico)

New Mexico has only three [Interstate Highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Interstate_Highways_in_New_Mexico): [Interstate 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_10_in_New_Mexico) travels southwest from [the Arizona](21883824.md) state line near [Lordsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lordsburg,_New_Mexico) to the area between [Las Cruces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Cruces,_New_Mexico) and [Anthony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony,_New_Mexico), near [El Paso, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Paso,_Texas); [Interstate 25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_25_in_New_Mexico) is a major north–south interstate highway starting from Las Cruces to the [Colorado](5399.md) state line near [Raton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raton,_New_Mexico); and [Interstate 40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_40_in_New_Mexico) is a major east–west interstate highway starting from the Arizona state line west of [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_New_Mexico) to the Texas state line east from [Tucumcari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucumcari,_New_Mexico). In [Albuquerque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque,_New_Mexico), I-25 and I-40 meet at a stack interchange called [The Big I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_I). The state is tied with Delaware, North Dakota, Puerto Rico, and Rhode Island in having the fewest primary interstate routes, which is partly a reflection of its rugged geography and sparse population.[^cite_note-257]

New Mexico currently has [15 United States Highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._Routes_in_New_Mexico), which account for over 2,980 miles (4,800 km) of its highway system. All but seven of its 33 counties are served by U.S. routes, with most of the remainder connected by Interstate Highways. Most routes were built in 1926 by the state government and are still managed and maintained by state or local authorities. The longest is [U.S. 70](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_70_in_New_Mexico), which spans over 448 miles (721 km) across southern New Mexico, making up roughly 15% of the state's total U.S. Highway length; the shortest is [U.S. 160](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_160_in_New_Mexico), which runs just 0.86 miles (1.38 km) across the [northwestern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Corners) corner of the state, between the [Arizona](21883824.md) and [Colorado](5399.md) borders.

The most famous route in New Mexico, if not the United States, was [U.S. 66](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_66), colloquially known as the nation's "Mother Road" for its scenic beauty and importance to migrants fleeing West from the [Dust Bowl](59749.md) of the 1930s.[^cite_note-258] The road crossed through [northern New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_New_Mexico), connecting the cities of [Albuquerque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque,_New_Mexico) and [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_New_Mexico), before being replaced by I-40 in 1985. Much of U.S. 66 remains in use for tourism and has been preserved for historical significance.[^cite_note-259] Another famous route was [U.S. 666](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_666), which ran south to north along the western portion of the state, serving the Four Corners area. It was known as the "Devil's Highway" due to the number [666](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/666_\(number\)) denoting the "[Number of the beast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Number_of_the_beast)" in Christianity; this numerical designation, as well as its high fatality rate, was subject to controversy, superstition, and numerous cultural references. U.S. 666 was subsequently renamed U.S. Route 491 in 2003. Many existing and former highways in New Mexico are recognized for their aesthetic, cultural, or historical significance, particularly for tourism purposes.[^cite_note-260] The state hosts ten out of 184 "America's Byways", which are federally designated for preservation due to their scenic beauty or national importance.[^cite_note-261]

### Rail

See also: [List of New Mexico railroads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_New_Mexico_railroads)



The [Cumbres and Toltec Scenic Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumbres_and_Toltec_Scenic_Railroad)

There were 2,354 route miles of railroads in 2000; this number increased by a few miles with the opening of the [Rail Runner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Rail_Runner_Express)'s extension to Santa Fe in 2006.[^cite_note-bts.gov2-262] In addition to local railroads and other tourist lines, the state jointly owns and operates a heritage [narrow-gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrow_gauge) steam railroad, the [Cumbres and Toltec Scenic Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumbres_and_Toltec_Scenic_Railway), with the state of [Colorado](5399.md) since 1970. Narrow-gauge railroads once connected many communities in the northern part of the state, from [Farmington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farmington,_New_Mexico) to Santa Fe.[^cite_note-Myrick-263]<sup>: 110 </sup> No fewer than 100 railroads of various names and lineage have operated in the state at some point.[^cite_note-Myrick-263]<sup>: 8 </sup> New Mexico's rail transportation system peaked in length after it became a U.S. state; in 1914, eleven railroads operated 3,124 route miles.[^cite_note-Myrick-263]<sup>: 10 </sup>

&#x20;Railroad surveyors arrived in New Mexico in the 1850s shortly after it became a U.S. territory.[^cite_note-mapping-264] The first railroads incorporated in 1869,[^cite_note-Myrick-263]<sup>: 9 </sup> and the first railway became operational in 1878: the [Atchison, Topeka & Santa Fe Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atchison,_Topeka_&_Santa_Fe_Railway) (ATSF), which entered via the lucrative and contested [Raton Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raton_Pass). The ATSF reached [El Paso, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Paso,_Texas) in 1881, and with the entry of the [Southern Pacific Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Pacific_Railroad) from the [Arizona Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Arizona) in 1880, created the nation's [second transcontinental railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcontinental_railroad#United_States), with a junction at [Deming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deming,_New_Mexico).[^cite_note-Myrick-263]<sup>: 9, 18, 58–59 </sup>[^cite_note-mapping-264] The [Denver & Rio Grande Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver_&_Rio_Grande_Railway), which generally used [narrow gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrow_gauge) equipment in New Mexico, entered the territory from [Colorado](5399.md), beginning service to [Española](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espa%C3%B1ola,_New_Mexico) in December 1880.[^cite_note-Myrick-263]<sup>: 95–96 </sup>[^cite_note-mapping-264] These first railroads were built as long-distance corridors; later railroad construction also targeted resource extraction.[^cite_note-Myrick-263]<sup>: 8–11 </sup>



The railway station in [Tucumcari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucumcari,_New_Mexico)

&#x20;The rise of [rail transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transportation) was a major source of demographic and economic growth, with many settlements expanding or established shortly thereafter. As early as 1878, the ATSF promoted [tourism in the region](#Tourism) with an emphasis on Native American imagery.[^cite_note-Richards2-265]<sup>: 64 </sup> [Named trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Named_train) often reflected the territory they traveled: *[Super Chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Chief)*, the streamlined successor to the *Chief*;[^cite_note-Richards2-265] *[Navajo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo_\(passenger_train\))*, an early transcontinental tourist train; and *[Cavern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavern_\(passenger_train\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)*, a through car operation connecting [Clovis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clovis,_New_Mexico) and [Carlsbad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlsbad,_New_Mexico) (by the early 1950s as train 23–24), were some of the named [passenger trains of the ATSF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atchison,_Topeka_and_Santa_Fe_Railway#Passenger_service) that connoted New Mexico,[^cite_note-Myrick-263]<sup>: 49–50 </sup>[^cite_note-Dorin-266]<sup>: 51 </sup> The *Super Chief* became a favorite of early Hollywood stars and among the most famous named trains in the U.S.; it was known for its luxury and exoticness, with cars bearing the name of regional Native American tribes and outfitted with the artwork of many local artists, but also for its speed, taking as little as 39 hours 45 minutes to reach Los Angeles from Chicago.[^cite_note-Richards2-265]



The [New Mexico Rail Runner Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Rail_Runner_Express) is a commuter operation that runs along the Central Rio Grande Valley.

&#x20;At its height, passenger train service connected nine of New Mexico's present [ten most populous cities](#Settlements) (the sole exception is [Rio Rancho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Rancho,_New_Mexico)); only [Albuquerque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque) and [Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico) are still connected by rail.[^cite_note-nmrailrunner.com-267] With the decline of most [intercity rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercity_rail) service in the U.S. in the late 1960s, New Mexico was left with minimal services; no less than six daily long-distance roundtrip trains, supplemented by many branch-line and local trains, served New Mexico in the early 1960s. Declines in passenger revenue, but not necessarily ridership, prompted many railroads to turn over their passenger services in truncated form to [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak), a state-owned enterprise. Amtrak, also known as the National Passenger Railroad Corporation, began operating the two extant long-distance routes on May 1, 1971.[^cite_note-Myrick-263][^cite_note-Richards2-265][^cite_note-Dorin-266] Resurrection of passenger rail service from [Denver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver) to [El Paso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Paso,_Texas), a route once plied in part by the ATSF's *El Pasoan*,[^cite_note-Dorin-266]<sup>: 37 </sup> has been proposed; in the 1980s, Governor [Toney Anaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toney_Anaya) suggested building a [high-speed rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail) line connecting the two cities with New Mexico's major cities.[^cite_note-268] In 2004, the Colorado-based nonprofit [Front Range Commuter Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_Range_Commuter_Rail) was established, with the goal of connecting [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming) and New Mexico with high-speed rail;[^cite_note-269] it became inactive in 2011.[^cite_note-270]



Downtown Santa Fe train station

Since 2006, a state-owned, privately run [commuter railway](6286.md), the [New Mexico Rail Runner Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Rail_Runner_Express), has served the [Albuquerque metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque_metropolitan_area), connecting the city proper with Santa Fe and other communities.[^cite_note-nmrailrunner.com-267][^cite_note-Holmes-271] The system expanded in 2008 with the addition of the [BNSF Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNSF_Railway)'s line from [Belen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belen,_New_Mexico) to a few miles south of Lamy.[^cite_note-272] Phase II of Rail Runner extended the line northward to [Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico) from the [Sandoval County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandoval_County/US_550_\(Rail_Runner_station\)) station, the northernmost station under Phase I service; the service now connects [Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_County), [Sandoval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandoval_County), [Bernalillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernalillo_County), and [Valencia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valencia_County) counties. Rail Runner operates scheduled service seven days per week,[^cite_note-273] connecting Albuquerque's population base and central business district to downtown Santa Fe with up to eight roundtrips in a day; the section of the line running south to [Belen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belen,_New_Mexico) is served less frequently.[^cite_note-274]

Amtrak's *[Southwest Chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Chief)* passes through daily at stations in [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_New_Mexico), Albuquerque, [Lamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamy,_New_Mexico), [Las Vegas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas,_New_Mexico), and [Raton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raton,_New_Mexico), offering connections to Los Angeles, Chicago and intermediate points.[^cite_note-275] A successor to the *Super Chief* and *[El Capitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Capitan_\(passenger_train\)),*[^cite_note-Dorin-266]<sup>: 115 </sup> the *Southwest Chief* is permitted a maximum speed of 90 mph (140 km/h) in various places on the tracks of the [BNSF Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNSF_Railway);[^cite_note-276] it also operates on [New Mexico Rail Runner Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Rail_Runner_Express) trackage. The *[Sunset Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunset_Limited)* makes stops three times a week in both directions at [Lordsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lordsburg,_New_Mexico), and [Deming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deming,_New_Mexico), serving Los Angeles, New Orleans and intermediate points.[^cite_note-277] The *Sunset Limited* is the successor to the [Southern Pacific Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Pacific_Railroad)'s train of the same name and operates exclusively on [Union Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Pacific) trackage in New Mexico.

New Mexico is served by two of the nation's ten [class I railroads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_I_railroad), which denote the highest revenue railways for freight: the [BNSF Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNSF_Railway) and the [Union Pacific Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Pacific_Railroad). Together, they operate 2,200 route miles of railway in the state.[^cite_note-bts.gov2-262]

### Aerospace

See also: [List of airports in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_New_Mexico)

New Mexico has four [primary commercial airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_New_Mexico) that are served by most major domestic and international airliners. [Albuquerque International Sunport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque_International_Sunport) is the state's main [aerial port of entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_entry) and by far the largest airport: It is the only one designated a medium-sized hub by the [Federal Aviation Administration](11186.md), serving millions of passengers annually.



Spaceport America terminal, *The Gateway*

The only other comparatively large airports are [Lea County Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lea_County_Regional_Airport), [Roswell International Air Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roswell_International_Air_Center), and [Santa Fe Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_Regional_Airport), which have varying degrees of service by major airlines. Most airports in New Mexico are small, [general aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_aviation) hubs operated by municipal and county governments, and usually served solely by local and regional [commuter airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_airliner).

Due to its sparse population and many isolated, rural communities, New Mexico ranks among the states most reliant on [Essential Air Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essential_Air_Service), a federal program that maintains a minimal level of scheduled air service to communities that are otherwise unprofitable for commercial airlines.

#### Spaceport America

New Mexico hosts the world's first operational and purpose-built commercial [spaceport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceport), [Spaceport America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceport_America), located in [Upham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upham,_New_Mexico), near [Truth or Consequences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truth_or_Consequences,_New_Mexico).[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-BizWeek-279][^cite_note-Discovery-280] It is operated by the state-backed [New Mexico Spaceport Authority (NMSA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Spaceport_Authority). [Rocket launches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_launch) began in April 2007,[^cite_note-Discovery-280] with the spaceport officially opening in 2011.[^cite_note-281] Tenants include [HAPSMobile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HAPSMobile), [UP Aerospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UP_Aerospace), [SpinLaunch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpinLaunch), and [Virgin Galactic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Galactic).[^cite_note-282]

Over 300 [suborbital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suborbital) flights have been successfully launched from Spaceport America since 2006, with the most notable being Virgin Galactic's [VSS *Unity*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VSS_Unity) on May 22, 2021, which made New Mexico the third U.S. state to launch humans into space, after California and Florida.[^cite_note-Robinson-Avila_2021-283][^cite_note-284]

On October 22, 2021, Spaceport America was the site of the first successfully tested vacuum-sealed "suborbital accelerator", which aims to offer a significantly more economical alternative to launching satellites via rockets.[^cite_note-Houser-2021-285] Conducted by Spaceport tenant SpinLaunch, the test is the first of roughly 30 demonstrations being planned.[^cite_note-Houser-2021-285]

## Government and politics

Main article: [Government of New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_New_Mexico)



Governor [Michelle Lujan Grisham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Lujan_Grisham) (D)

The [Constitution of New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_New_Mexico) was adopted by [popular referendum](51582.md) in 1911. It establishes a [republican form of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_form_of_government) based on [popular sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_sovereignty) and a [separation of powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers). New Mexico has a [bill of rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_of_rights) modeled on its [federal counterpart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights), but with more expansive rights and freedoms; for example, victims of certain serious crimes, such as aggravated battery and sexual assault, have explicit rights to privacy, dignity, and the timely adjudication of their case.[^cite_note-286] Major state issues may be decided by popular vote, and the constitution may be amended by a majority vote of both lawmakers and the electorate.[^cite_note-ReferenceA1-287]

### Governmental structure

[Mirroring the federal system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_Four_of_the_United_States_Constitution), the New Mexico government consists of executive, legislative, and judicial departments. The executive is led by the [governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_New_Mexico) and other popularly elected officials, including the [lieutenant governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_New_Mexico) (elected on the same ticket as the governor), [attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_of_New_Mexico), [secretary of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_New_Mexico), [state auditor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_State_Auditor), [state treasurer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_State_Treasurer), and [commissioner of public lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Commissioner_of_Public_Lands). New Mexico's governor is granted more authority than those of other states, with the power to appoint most high-ranking officials in the cabinet and other state agencies.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-23]

The legislative branch consists of the bicameral [New Mexico Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Legislature), comprising the 70-member [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_House_of_Representatives) and the 42-member [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Senate). Members of the House are elected to two-year terms, while those of the Senate are elected every four years. New Mexican legislators are unique in the U.S. for being volunteers, receiving only a daily stipend while in session; this "citizen legislature" dates back to New Mexico's admission as a state, and is considered a source of civic pride.[^cite_note-288]

The judiciary is headed by the [New Mexico Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Supreme_Court), the state's highest court, which primarily [adjudicates appeals](643.md) from lower courts or government agencies. It is made up of five judges popularly elected every eight years with overlapping terms. Below the state supreme court is the [New Mexico Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Court_of_Appeals), which has intermediate appellate jurisdiction statewide. New Mexico has 13 judicial districts with circuit courts of [general jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_jurisdiction), as well as various municipal, [magistrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_court_magistrate_judge), and probate courts of [limited jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited_jurisdiction).

New Mexico is organized into numerous local governments consisting of counties, municipalities, and special districts.[^cite_note-289]

### Politics

See also: [Elections in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_New_Mexico), [Political party strength in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party_strength_in_New_Mexico), and [New Mexico Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Legislature)

Since 2018, New Mexico has been led by Governor [Michelle Lujan Grisham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Lujan_Grisham) and Lieutenant Governor [Howie Morales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howie_Morales), both of the [Democratic Party](5043544.md). All constitutional officers are currently Democrats, including Secretary of State [Maggie Toulouse Oliver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maggie_Toulouse_Oliver),[^cite_note-290] Attorney General [Raúl Torrez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ra%C3%BAl_Torrez),[^cite_note-291] State Auditor [Joseph Maestas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Maestas),[^cite_note-292] State Land Commissioner [Stephanie Garcia Richard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephanie_Garcia_Richard),[^cite_note-293] and State Treasurer [Laura Montaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Montoya_\(politician\)).[^cite_note-294]

| Party | Number  of voters | Fraction |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | [Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_New_Mexico) | 575,726 | 39.07% |
|  | [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_of_New_Mexico) | 444,654 | 30.06% |
|  | [Independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_voter) / [other](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_the_United_States) | 403,396 | 27.80% |
|  | [Libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_Party_of_New_Mexico) | 27,205 | 1.90 |
|  | Total | 1,450,981 | 100.0% |

Both chambers of the [New Mexico Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Legislature) have Democratic majorities: 27 Democrats and 15 Republicans in the Senate, and 45 Democrats and 25 Republicans in the House of Representatives. Likewise, the state is represented in the [U.S. Senate](24909346.md) by Democrats [Martin Heinrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Heinrich) and [Ben Ray Luján](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Ray_Luj%C3%A1n). The state's three delegates to the [U.S. House of Representatives](19468510.md) are Democrats [Melanie Stansbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanie_Stansbury), [Gabe Vasquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabe_Vasquez), and [Teresa Leger Fernandez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teresa_Leger_Fernandez), representing the first, second, and third districts, respectively.



[2024 U.S. presidential election results](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_presidential_election_in_New_Mexico) by county in New Mexico&#x20;

&#x20; Democratic

&#x20; Republican

Since achieving statehood in [1912](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1912_United_States_presidential_election), New Mexico has been carried by the national popular vote winner in every presidential election except in [1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_United_States_presidential_election) and [2024](59568546.md).[^cite_note-296] Until 2008, New Mexico was a [swing state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state) in presidential elections. The [1992 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election) of Bill Clinton marked the first time the state was won by a Democrat since Lyndon B. Johnson in 1964. [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore) narrowly carried the state in [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election), and [George W. Bush](3414021.md) won in [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_United_States_presidential_election) by less than 6,000 votes. The election of [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) in [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election) marked the state's transition into [a Democratic stronghold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_states_and_blue_states); Obama was also the first Democrat to win a majority of New Mexico votes since Johnson.[^cite_note-Weigel-297] Obama won New Mexico again in 2012, followed by Hillary Clinton in 2016, Joe Biden in 2020, and Kamala Harris in 2024.

Although state politics are decidedly Democratic-leaning, New Mexico's political culture is relatively moderate and bipartisan by national standards. While registered Democrats outnumber registered Republicans by nearly 200,000, New Mexico voters have historically favored moderate to conservative candidates of both parties at the state and federal levels: according to [Pew Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), the largest political ideology among New Mexicans is [political moderate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_moderate) at 36%, while 34% are [conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States), 23% are [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_in_the_United_States), and 7% stated they did not know.[^cite_note-Pew_Research_Center's_Religion_&_Public_Life_Project_2022-298] Likewise, New Mexico's demographics are atypical of most traditional liberal states with "political ideology less important" than candidates' profiles or outreach efforts.[^cite_note-Caldwell-2022-299] Due to their historically positive connections to the state's heritage, the [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_of_New_Mexico) and [Democratic parties of New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_New_Mexico) are each relatively robust, and New Mexico is considered a [bellwether state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellwether_\(politics\)).[^cite_note-The_Advocate_20202-300][^cite_note-Reichbach_20152-301][^cite_note-Alberta_20202-302][^cite_note-Ostermeier_20112-303][^cite_note-Broh_1980_pp._564–5702-304] The state's [Republican Party](32070.md) was the first to incorporate Hispanics and Natives into leadership roles, such as territorial governor [Miguel Antonio Otero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Antonio_Otero_\(born_1859\)) and state governor [Octaviano Ambrosio Larrazolo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octaviano_Ambrosio_Larrazolo), later the first Mexican American and first Hispanic member of the U.S. Senate.[^cite_note-McClain_2018_p._1882-305][^cite_note-Hornung_2013_p._832-306] Republican president [Theodore Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt) greatly respected the Hispanos, Mexican Americans, and indigenous communities of New Mexico, many of whom had been part of his [Rough Riders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rough_Riders).[^cite_note-Robertson_20112-307][^cite_note-Weideman_20222-308]

Lujan Grisham succeeded two-term Republican governor [Susana Martinez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susana_Martinez) on January 1, 2019. [Gary Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Johnson) was governor from 1995 to 2003 as a Republican, but in [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election) and [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) ran for president as the [Libertarian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_Party_\(United_States\)) nominee. New Mexico's Second Congressional District is among the most competitive in the country: Republican Herrell narrowly lost to [Democrat](5043544.md) [Xochitl Torres Small](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xochitl_Torres_Small) in 2018, retook her seat in 2020, and lost to Democrat [Gabe Vasquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabe_Vasquez) in 2022.[^cite_note-Caldwell-2022-299] Election cycles within the past decade have seen moderate incumbents replaced by progressive Democrats in cities like Albuquerque, Santa Fe, and Las Cruces, with conservative Republicans elected in rural areas. Democrats are usually strongest in the [Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico) area, parts of the [Albuquerque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque,_New_Mexico) metro area (such as the southeast and central areas, including the affluent Nob Hill neighborhood and the vicinity of the [University of New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_New_Mexico)), Northern and West Central New Mexico, and [Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) reservations, especially the [Navajo Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo_Nation).[^cite_note-Weigel-297] Republicans have traditionally had strongholds in the state's east and south, the [Farmington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farmington,_New_Mexico) area, [Rio Rancho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Rancho,_New_Mexico), and the newly developed areas in the northwest mesa. Albuquerque's Northeast Heights have historically leaned Republican but have recently become a key swing area for Democrats.

A 2020 study ranked New Mexico as the 20th-hardest state for citizens to vote in, primarily due to the inaccessibility of polling stations for many isolated communities.[^cite_note-309]

#### Female minority representation

New Mexico has elected more [women of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_of_color) to public office than any other U.S. state.[^cite_note-Thomson-DeVeaux-2020-310] While the trend is partly reflective of the state's disproportionately high Hispanic and indigenous populations, it also reflects longstanding cultural and political trends: In 1922, [Soledad Chávez Chacón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soledad_C._Chac%C3%B3n) was the first woman elected secretary of state of New Mexico, and the first Hispanic woman elected to statewide office in the United States. [Republican](32070.md) governor [Susana Martinez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susana_Martinez) was the first Hispanic female governor in the United States, and [Democrat](5043544.md) congresswoman [Deb Haaland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deb_Haaland) was among the first Native American women elected to the U.S. Congress.[^cite_note-Time_2017-311][^cite_note-Becker_2019-312]

Research by the [Center for American Women and Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_American_Women_and_Politics) at [Rutgers University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutgers_University) found that two-thirds of all nonwhite women who have ever been elected governor in the U.S. are from New Mexico, including the current governor, Lujan Grisham. The state also accounts for nearly one-third of the women of color who have served in any statewide executive office, such as lieutenant governor and secretary of state, a distinction shared by only ten other states.[^cite_note-Thomson-DeVeaux-2020-310] New Mexico also has a relatively high percentage of state legislators who are women of color, which at 16% is the sixth highest in the nation.

New Mexico is described as a "national leader in electing female legislators".[^cite_note-McKay-2022-313] As of January 2023, it ranked sixth in the number of female state legislators (43.8%),[^cite_note-314] with women comprising a majority of the New Mexico House of Representatives (53%) and over a quarter of the Senate (29%).[^cite_note-McKay-2022-313] Women also hold a majority of seats on the state Supreme Court and the Court of Appeals.[^cite_note-McKay-2022-313] At the federal level, two out of three congressional districts are represented by women.

### Local government

Main article: [Local government in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_New_Mexico)

See also: [Government of Albuquerque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Albuquerque,_New_Mexico)

Local government in New Mexico consists primarily of [counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_New_Mexico) and [municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_settlements_in_New_Mexico). There are 33 counties, of which the most populous is [Bernalillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernalillo_County,_New_Mexico), which contains the state's largest city, [Albuquerque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque,_New_Mexico). Counties are usually governed by an elected five-member county commission, sheriff, assessor, clerk, and treasurer. A municipality may call itself a village, town, or city,[^cite_note-315] with no distinction in law and no correlation to any particular form of government. Municipal elections are non-partisan.[^cite_note-316] In addition, limited local authority can be vested in special districts and landowners' associations.

### Law

New Mexico is one of 23 states without the death penalty,[^cite_note-317] becoming the 15th state to abolish capital punishment in 2009.[^cite_note-318]
