The state has among the most permissive firearms laws in the country.[^cite_note-NRA-ILA-2020-320] Its constitution explicitly enshrines the right to bear arms and prevents local governments from regulating gun ownership.[^cite_note-321] Residents may purchase any firearm deemed legal under federal law without a permit.[^cite_note-NRA-ILA-2020-320] State law mandates a seven-day waiting period for picking up a firearm after it has been purchased (holders of concealed handgun licenses are exempt from the waiting period) and no restrictions on magazine capacity. New Mexico is a "shall-issue" state for [concealed carry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concealed_carry) permits, giving applicants the presumptive right to a license without giving a compelling reason.[^cite_note-322]

Before December 2013, New Mexico law was silent on [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States). The issuance of marriage licenses to same-sex couples was determined at the county level, with some county clerks issuing marriage licenses to same-sex couples and others not. In December 2013, the [New Mexico Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Supreme_Court) unanimously directed county clerks to issue marriage licenses to same-sex couples, thereby making New Mexico the 17th state to recognize same-sex marriage statewide.

Based on 2008 data, New Mexico had 146 law enforcement agencies across the state, county, and municipal levels.[^cite_note-Reaves-2011-323] State [law enforcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement) is statutorily administered by the Department of Public Safety (DPS).[^cite_note-324] The [New Mexico State Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_State_Police) is a division of the DPS with jurisdiction over all crimes in the state.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326] As of 2008, New Mexico had over 5,000 sworn police officers, a ratio of 252 per 100,000 residents, which is roughly the same as the nation.[^cite_note-Reaves-2011-323] The state struggles with one of the nation's highest rates of officer-involved killings, which has prompted political and legal reforms at local and state levels.[^cite_note-327]

In April 2021, New Mexico became the 18th state to [legalize cannabis for recreational use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legalization_of_non-medical_cannabis_in_the_United_States); possession, personal cultivation, and retail sales are permitted under certain conditions, while relevant marijuana-related arrests and convictions are expunged.[^cite_note-328] New Mexico has long pioneered loosening cannabis restrictions: In 1978, it was the first state to pass legislation allowing the [medical use of marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicinal_marijuana_in_the_United_States) in some form, albeit restricted to a federal research program.[^cite_note-329] In 1999, Republican Governor [Gary Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Johnson) became the highest-ranking elected official in the U.S. to publicly endorse drug legalization.[^cite_note-330] Medicinal marijuana was fully legalized in 2007, making New Mexico the 12th state to do so, and the fourth via legislative action.[^cite_note-331] In 2019, it was the first U.S. state to decriminalize possession of drug paraphernalia.[^cite_note-332]

As of June 2022, New Mexico has one of the nation's [most permissive abortion laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_law_in_the_United_States_by_state): elective abortion care is legal at all stages of pregnancy, without restrictions such as long waiting periods or parental consent.[^cite_note-333] In 2021, the state repealed a 1969 "[trigger law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trigger_law)" that had banned most abortion procedures, which would have come into effect after the U.S. Supreme Court's ruling in *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)*.[^cite_note-AAYVZYl-334] In response to the *Dobbs* decision, which held that abortion was not a constitutional right, New Mexico's governor issued an executive order protecting abortion providers from out-of-state litigation, in anticipation of the influx of nonresidents seeking abortions.[^cite_note-AAYVZYl-334][^cite_note-335]

### Fiscal policy

New Mexico's government has one of the largest state budgets per capita, at \$9,101 per resident.[^cite_note-336] As of 2017, the state had an [S\&P Global Rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S\&P_Global_Ratings) of AA+, denoting very strong capacity to meet financial commitments and very low credit risk.

New Mexico has two constitutionally mandated [permanent funds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_fund): The [Land Grant Permanent Fund (LGPF)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Land_Grant_Permanent_Fund), established upon statehood in 1912, and the Severance Tax Permanent Fund (STPF), created in 1973 during the [oil boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_boom).[^cite_note-NM_LFC-2021-337] Both funds derive revenue from rents, royalties, and bonuses related to the state's extensive oil, gas, and mining operations; the vast majority of the LGPF's distributions are earmarked for "common \[public] schools", while all distributions from the STPF are allocated to the LGPF.[^cite_note-NM_LFC-2021-337] As of 2020, the LGPF was valued at \$21.6 billion and the STPF at \$5.8 billion.[^cite_note-NM_LFC-2021-337]

## Education



The [New Mexico Public Education Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Public_Education_Department) is in Santa Fe.

Due to its relatively low population and numerous federally funded research facilities, New Mexico had the highest concentration of PhD holders of any state in 2000.[^cite_note-338] [Los Alamos County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Alamos_County,_New_Mexico), which hosts the [eponymous national laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Alamos_National_Laboratory), leads the state in the most post-secondary degree holders, at 38.7% of residents, or 4,899 of 17,950.[^cite_note-339] However, New Mexico routinely ranks near the bottom in studies measuring the quality of primary and secondary school education.[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-Chief-2021-341][^cite_note-342][^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344][^cite_note-345]

By national standards, New Mexico has one of the highest concentrations of persons who did not finish high school or have some college education, albeit by a low margin: Slightly more than 14% of residents did not have a high school diploma, compared to the national rate of 11.4%, the fifth lowest out of 52 U.S. states and territories. Almost a quarter of people over 25 (23.9%) did not complete college,[^cite_note-bber.unm.edu-120] compared with 21% nationally.[^cite_note-346] New Mexico ranks among the bottom ten states in the proportion of residents with a bachelor's degree or higher (27.7%), but 21st in PhD earners (12.2%); the national average is 33.1% and 12.8%, respectively. In 2020, the number of doctorate recipients was 300, placing the state 34th in the nation.[^cite_note-347]

In 2018, a state judge issued a landmark ruling that "New Mexico is violating the constitutional rights of at-risk students by failing to provide them with sufficient education", in particularly those with indigenous, non-English-speaking, and low-income backgrounds.[^cite_note-Mckay-2018-348] The court ordered the governor and legislature to provide an adequate system by April 2019;[^cite_note-349] in response, New Mexico increased teacher salaries, funded an extended school year, expanded prekindergarten childhood education programs, and developed a budget formula for delivering more funding to schools that serve at-risk and low-income students.[^cite_note-McKay-2021-350] Nevertheless, many activists and public officials contend that these efforts continue to fall short, particularly with respect to Native American schools and students.[^cite_note-McKay-2021-350]

### Primary and secondary education

See also: [List of school districts in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_school_districts_in_New_Mexico) and [List of high schools in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_schools_in_New_Mexico)

The [New Mexico Public Education Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Public_Education_Department) oversees the operation of primary and secondary schools; individual school districts directly operate and staff schools.

In January 2022, New Mexico became the first state in the U.S. to recruit national guardsmen and state workers to serve as substitute teachers due to staffing shortages caused by COVID-19.[^cite_note-351] Partly in response to pandemic-related shortages, on March 1, 2022, Governor Grisham signed into law four bills to increase the salaries and benefits of teachers and other school staff, particularly in entry-level positions.[^cite_note-352]

### Postsecondary education

See also: [List of colleges and universities in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_New_Mexico)



Public New Mexico colleges and universities. New Mexico Higher Education Department.

New Mexico has 41 accredited, degree-granting institutions; twelve are private and 29 are state-funded, including four tribal colleges.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355] Additionally, select students can attend certain institutions in Colorado, at in-state tuition rates, pursuant to a reciprocity program between the two states.[^cite_note-356]

Graduates of four-year colleges in New Mexico have some of the lowest student debt burdens in the U.S.; the class of 2017 owed an average of \$21,237 compared with a national average of \$28,650, according to the [Institute for College Access & Success](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Institute_for_College_Access_and_Success).[^cite_note-Romero-2019-357]

New Mexico ranked 13th in the 2022 Social Mobility Index (SMI), which measures the extent to which economically disadvantaged students (with family incomes below the national median) have access to colleges and universities with lower tuition and indebtedness and higher job prospects.[^cite_note-358]

#### Major flagship (R1) universities

- [University of New Mexico at Albuquerque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_New_Mexico)

- [New Mexico State University at Las Cruces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_State_University)

#### Regional state universities

- [New Mexico Institute of Mining & Technology at Socorro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Tech)

- [Eastern New Mexico University at Portales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_New_Mexico_University)

- [New Mexico Highlands University at Las Vegas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Highlands_University)

- [Western New Mexico University at Silver City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_New_Mexico_University)

#### Lottery scholarship

New Mexico is one of eight states that fund college scholarships through the state [lottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lottery).[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-Bryan-2018b-360][^cite_note-Peterson-2019-361] The state requires that the [lottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Lottery) put 30% of its gross sales into the scholarship fund.[^cite_note-362] The scholarship is available to residents who graduated from a state high school and attend a state university full-time while maintaining a GPA of 2.5 or higher.[^cite_note-363] It covered 100% of tuition when it was instated in 1996,[^cite_note-Bryan-2018a-364] decreased to 90%, then dropped to 60% in 2017.[^cite_note-Bryan-2018b-360] The value slightly increased in 2018, and new legislation was passed to outline what funds are available per type of institution.[^cite_note-Bryan-2018a-364]

#### Opportunity scholarship

In 2019, New Mexico announced a plan to make tuition at its public colleges and universities free for all state residents, regardless of family income.[^cite_note-Romero-2019-357] The proposal was described as going further than any other existing state or federal plan or program at the time.[^cite_note-Romero-2019-357] In March 2022, New Mexico became the first state to offer free college tuition for all residents. The legislature passed a bipartisan bill allocating almost 1% of the budget to tuition and fees at all 29 public colleges, universities, community colleges, and tribal colleges.[^cite_note-auto1-365] The program, which took effect on July 1, 2022, has been called among the nation's most ambitious and generous, as it is available to all residents regardless of income, work status, or legal status, and is provided without taking into account other scholarships and sources of financial aid.[^cite_note-auto1-365]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_New_Mexico)

See also: [List of people from New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_from_New_Mexico), [New Mexican cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexican_cuisine), [New Mexico chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_chile), [New Mexico wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_wine), [List of breweries in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_breweries_in_New_Mexico), [Music of New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_New_Mexico), and [New Mexico music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_music)



Symbols of the Southwest: a string of dried [chile pepper pods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chili_pepper) (a [ristra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ristra)) and a bleached white [cow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle)'s [skull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skull) hang in a market near [Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico).

New Mexican culture is a fusion of indigenous, Spanish, Hispanic, and American influences. The intermingling of these diverse groups is reflected in New Mexico's demographics, [toponyms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toponymy), cuisine, dialect, and identity. Compared to other Western states, New Mexico's Spanish and Mexican heritage remain more visible and enduring, due to it having been the oldest, most populous, and most important province in New Spain's northern periphery.[^cite_note-366] Historians allege this history has been understated or marginalized by persistent [American biases and misconceptions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Legend_\(Spain\)) about Spanish colonial history, reflected in part by the fact that many Americans do not know it is part of the U.S.;[^cite_note-367] this misconception variably elicits frustration, amusement, or even pride among New Mexicans as evidence of their unique heritage.[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370] The Flag of New Mexico contains the [Zuni sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zuni_mythology), but with four rays rather than three.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373]

Like other states in the [American Southwest](179571.md), New Mexico bears the legacy of the "[Old West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_frontier)" period of American westward expansion, characterized by [cattle ranching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle_ranching), [cowboys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cowboy), [pioneers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_pioneer), the [Santa Fe Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_Trail), and conflicts among and between settlers and Native Americans.[^cite_note-Beck-McNamee-2023b-141] The state's vast and diverse geography, sparse population, and [abundance of ghost towns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ghost_towns_in_New_Mexico) have contributed to its enduring frontier image and atmosphere.[^cite_note-Beck-McNamee-2023b-141] Many fictional works of the [Western genre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_\(genre\)) are [set or produced in New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_films_shot_in_New_Mexico).

### Architecture

Main articles: [Pueblo architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pueblo_architecture), [Territorial Style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_Style), [New Mexico vernacular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_vernacular), [Pueblo Revival architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pueblo_Revival_architecture), and [Territorial Revival architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_Revival_architecture)

Examples of New Mexico's architectural history date back to the [Ancestral Puebloans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestral_Puebloans) within [Oasisamerica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oasisamerica). The [Hispanos of New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanos_of_New_Mexico) adapted the [Pueblo architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pueblo_architecture) style within their own buildings, and following the establishment of [Albuquerque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque,_New_Mexico) in 1706, the [Territorial Style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_Style) of architecture blended the styles.[^cite_note-Shapland_2018-374] Rural communities incorporated both building types into a [New Mexico vernacular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_vernacular) style, further exemplifying the indigenous roots of New Mexico.[^cite_note-Shacklette_2012_pp._157–176-375] After statehood, the modern [Pueblo Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pueblo_Revival_architecture) and [Territorial Revival architectural styles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_Revival_architecture) became more prevalent, with these revival architectures becoming officially encouraged since the 1930s.[^cite_note-Nelson_2021-376] These styles have been blended with other modern styles, as happened with [Pueblo Deco architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pueblo_Deco_architecture),[^cite_note-Secord_2012-377] within modern contemporary New Mexican architecture.[^cite_note-Thompson_Dunn_2021_p._34-378][^cite_note-Keates_2019-379]

### Art, literature, and media

See also: [Art of the American Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_the_American_Southwest); [New Mexican literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexican_literature); and [Media in Albuquerque, New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_in_Albuquerque,_New_Mexico)

The earliest New Mexico artists whose work survives today are the [Mimbres Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimbres_culture), whose black and white pottery could be mistaken for modern art, except for the fact that it was produced before AD 1130. Many examples of this work can be seen at the [Deming Luna Mimbres Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deming_Armory)[^cite_note-380] and at the [Western New Mexico University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_New_Mexico_University) Museum.[^cite_note-381]

[Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico) has long hosted a thriving artistic community, which has included such prominent figures as [Bruce Nauman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Nauman), [Richard Tuttle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tuttle), [John Connell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Connell_\(artist\)), and [Steina Vasulka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steina_Vasulka). The capital city has several art museums, including the [New Mexico Museum of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Museum_of_Art), Museum of Spanish Colonial Art, [Museum of International Folk Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_International_Folk_Art), [Museum of Indian Arts and Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Indian_Arts_and_Culture), [Museum of Contemporary Native Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Contemporary_Native_Art), [SITE Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SITE_Santa_Fe) and others. Colonies for artists and writers thrive, and the small city teems with art galleries. In August, the city hosts the annual [Santa Fe Indian Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_Indian_Market), which is the oldest and largest juried Native American art showcase in the world. Performing arts include the [Santa Fe Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_Opera), which presents five operas in repertory each July to August; the [Santa Fe Chamber Music Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_Chamber_Music_Festival) held each summer; and the restored [Lensic Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lensic_Theater), a principal venue for many kinds of performances. The weekend after Labor Day boasts the burning of [Zozobra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zozobra), a fifty-foot (15 m) marionette, during [Fiestas de Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiestas_de_Santa_Fe).



Interior of the Crosby Theater at the [Santa Fe Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_Opera), viewed from the mezzanine

As New Mexico's largest city, Albuquerque hosts many of the state's leading cultural events and institutions, including the [New Mexico Museum of Natural History and Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Museum_of_Natural_History_and_Science), the [National Hispanic Cultural Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Hispanic_Cultural_Center), the [National Museum of Nuclear Science & History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Nuclear_Science_&_History), and the famed annual [Albuquerque International Balloon Fiesta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque_International_Balloon_Fiesta). The National Hispanic Cultural Center has held hundreds of performing arts events, art showcases, and other events related to Spanish culture in New Mexico and worldwide in the centerpiece Roy E Disney Center for the Performing Arts or in other venues at the 53-acre facility. New Mexico residents and visitors alike can enjoy performing arts from around the world at Popejoy Hall on the campus of the University of New Mexico. Popejoy Hall hosts singers, dancers, Broadway shows, other types of acts, and Shakespeare.[^cite_note-382] Albuquerque also has the iconic [KiMo Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KiMo_Theater) built in 1927 in the [Pueblo Revival Style architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pueblo_Revival_Style_architecture). The KiMo presents live theater and concerts as well as movies and [simulcast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simulcast) operas.[^cite_note-383] In addition to other general interest theaters, Albuquerque also has the African American Performing Arts Center and Exhibit Hall which showcases achievements by people of African descent[^cite_note-384] and the [Indian Pueblo Cultural Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Pueblo_Cultural_Center) which highlights the cultural heritage of the [First Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) people of New Mexico.[^cite_note-385]



[Luminarias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luminarias) in the old mission church, [Jemez State Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jemez_State_Monument)&#x20;

New Mexico holds strong to its Spanish heritage. Old Spanish traditions such [zarzuelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zarzuela) and [flamenco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flamenco) are popular;[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387] the University of New Mexico is the only institute of higher education in the world with a program dedicated to flamenco.[^cite_note-388] Flamenco dancer and native New Mexican [María Benítez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Ben%C3%ADtez) founded the Maria Benítez Institute for Spanish Arts "to present programs of the highest quality of the rich artistic heritage of Spain, as expressed through music, dance, visual arts, and other art forms". There is also the annual Festival Flamenco Internacional de Alburquerque, where native Spanish and New Mexican flamenco dancers perform at the University of New Mexico; it is the largest and oldest flamenco event outside of Spain.[^cite_note-389]

In the mid-20th century, there was a thriving Hispano school of literature and scholarship being produced in both English and Spanish. Among the more notable authors were [Angélico Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ang%C3%A9lico_Ch%C3%A1vez), [Nina Otero-Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adelina_Otero-Warren), Fabiola Cabeza de Baca, Aurelio Espinosa, [Cleofas Jaramillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleofas_Mart%C3%ADnez_Jaramillo), [Juan Bautista Rael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Bautista_Rael), and [Aurora Lucero-White Lea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurora_Lucero-White_Lea). [D. H. Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D._H._Lawrence) lived near [Taos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taos,_New_Mexico) in the 1920s, at the [D. H. Lawrence Ranch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D._H._Lawrence_Ranch), where there is a shrine said to contain his ashes.

New Mexico's strong [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Spain), [Anglo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-America), and [Wild West frontier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_West) motifs have contributed to a unique [body of literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexican_literature), represented by internationally recognized authors such as [Rudolfo Anaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolfo_Anaya), [Tony Hillerman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Hillerman), and [Daniel Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Abraham_\(author\)).[^cite_note-390] [Western fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_fiction) folk heroes [Billy the Kid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_the_Kid), [Elfego Baca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elfego_Baca), [Geronimo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geronimo), and [Pat Garrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Garrett) originate in New Mexico.[^cite_note-New_Mexico_Archives_Online_2022-391] These same Hispanic, indigenous, and frontier histories have given New Mexico a place in the history of [country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_music) and [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_music_\(North_America\)) music,[^cite_note-New_Mexico_Tourism_&_Travel_2019-392][^cite_note-Elizondo_2022-393][^cite_note-Segarra_2022-394] with its own [New Mexico music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_music) genre,[^cite_note-Nashville_To_New_Mexico_2022-395][^cite_note-Arellano_2017-396][^cite_note-Energy.gov_2020-397] exemplified by [Al Hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Hurricane),[^cite_note-Interns_2017-398] [Robert Mirabal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Mirabal),[^cite_note-The_Taos_News_2019-399] and [Michael Martin Murphey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Martin_Murphey).[^cite_note-Doland_2018-400]

[Silver City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver_City,_New_Mexico), originally a mining town, is a major hub and exhibition center for artists, visual and otherwise.[^cite_note-401] Another former mining town turned art haven is [Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid,_New_Mexico), which gained fame as the filming location for the 2007 movie *[Wild Hogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_Hogs)*.[^cite_note-402] Las Cruces has a museum system affiliated with the Smithsonian Institution Affiliations Program[^cite_note-403] and hosts various cultural and artistic opportunities for residents and visitors.[^cite_note-404]

The [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_\(genre\)) genre immortalized the varied mountainous, riparian, and desert environment on film.[^cite_note-2006_Momaday-405] Owing to a combination of financial incentives, low cost, and geographic diversity, New Mexico has long been a popular setting or filming location for films and television series. In addition to *Wild Hogs*, movies filmed in New Mexico include *[Sunshine Cleaning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunshine_Cleaning)* and *[Vampires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vampires_\(1998_film\))*. Some seasons of the [A\&E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A\&E_\(TV_channel\))/[Netflix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netflix) series *[Longmire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longmire_\(TV_series\))* were filmed in New Mexico, including in [Las Vegas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas,_New_Mexico), [Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico), [Eagle Nest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eagle_Nest,_New_Mexico), and [Red River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_River,_New_Mexico).[^cite_note-406] The [*Breaking Bad* franchise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breaking_Bad_\(franchise\)) is set and was filmed in and around Albuquerque, a product of the ongoing success of [media in the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_in_Albuquerque,_New_Mexico) in large part helped by [Albuquerque Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque_Studios) and the presence of production studios like [Netflix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netflix) and [NBCUniversal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBCUniversal).[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-Bloomberg_2021-408][^cite_note-Padilla_NBCUniversal_2021-75]

### Cuisine

Main article: [New Mexican cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexican_cuisine)

New Mexico is known for its unique and eclectic culinary scene,[^cite_note-409] which fuses various [indigenous cuisines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_cuisine) with those of [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_cuisine) and [Mexican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_cuisine) [Hispanos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanos_of_New_Mexico) originating in [Nuevo México](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_de_Nuevo_M%C3%A9xico).[^cite_note-Enchantment-410][^cite_note-Swentzell_Perea_2016_p.-411][^cite_note-Nostrand_1996_p._13-412] Like other aspects of the state's culture, [New Mexican cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexican_cuisine) has been shaped by a variety of influences from throughout its history;[^cite_note-Taylor_2016_p._361-413][^cite_note-Enchantment-410][^cite_note-New_Mexico_Magazine_2012_p.-414] consequently, it is unlike [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_cuisine) food originating elsewhere in the [contiguous United States](83759.md).[^cite_note-Taco_USA-415]<sup>: 109 </sup>[^cite_note-Guide-416][^cite_note-Sutter_2017-417] Distinguishing characteristics include the use of local spices, herbs, flavors, and vegetables, particularly red and green [New Mexico chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_chile) peppers,[^cite_note-Video_2022-418][^cite_note-Tanis_2016-419][^cite_note-Larese_2013-420] [anise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anise) (used in [*bizcochitos*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bizcochito)),[^cite_note-Jamison_2013-421] and [*piñon*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine_nuts) (pine nuts).[^cite_note-Nut-422]

Among the dishes unique to New Mexico are [frybread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frybread)-style [*sopapillas*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sopapilla), [breakfast burritos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breakfast_burrito), *[enchilada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enchilada) montada* (stacked enchiladas), green chile stew, *[carne seca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carne_seca)* (a thinly sliced variant of [jerky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerky)), green chile burgers, *[posole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pozole)* (a [hominy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominy) dish), slow-cooked *frijoles* (beans, typically [pinto beans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinto_bean)), *calabacitas* (sautéed [zucchini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zucchini) and [summer squash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summer_squash)), and *[carne adovada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adobada#New_Mexico)* (pork marinated in red chile).[^cite_note-Matador_Network_2011-423][^cite_note-New_Mexico_Secretary_of_State_2018-424][^cite_note-Bizarre_Foods_Delicious_Destinations,_Albuquerque-425] The state is also the epicenter of a burgeoning Native American culinary movement, in which chefs of indigenous descent serve traditional cuisine through [food trucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_truck).[^cite_note-426]

### Sports



The [Santa Ana Star Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Ana_Star_Center)

No major league professional sports teams are based in New Mexico, but the [Albuquerque Isotopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque_Isotopes) are the [Pacific Coast League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Coast_League) baseball affiliate of the [MLB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLB) [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies). The state hosts several baseball teams of the [Pecos League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pecos_League): the [Roswell Invaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roswell_Invaders?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Ruidoso Osos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruidoso_Osos), [Santa Fe Fuego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe_Fuego?action=edit\&redlink=1), and the [White Sands Pupfish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Sands_Pupfish_\(baseball\)). The [Duke City Gladiators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_City_Gladiators) of the [Indoor Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indoor_Football_League) (IFL) plays their home games at [Tingley Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tingley_Coliseum) in Albuquerque; the city also hosts two soccer teams: [New Mexico United](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_United), which began playing in the [second tier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_soccer_league_system) [USL Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USL_Championship) in 2019, and the associated [New Mexico United U23](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_United_U23), which plays in the fourth tier [USL League Two](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USL_League_Two).

Collegiate athletics are the center of [spectator sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectator_sport) in New Mexico, namely the rivalry between various teams of the [University of New Mexico Lobos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Lobos) and the [New Mexico State Aggies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_State_Aggies).[^cite_note-427] The intense competition between the two teams is often referred to as the "[Rio Grande Rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Grande_Rivalry)" or the "Battle of [I-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_25_in_New_Mexico)" (both campuses are along that highway). [NMSU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NMSU) also has a rivalry with the [University of Texas at El Paso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_at_El_Paso) called "[The Battle of I-10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Battle_of_I-10)". The winner of the NMSU-UTEP football game receives the [Silver Spade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver_Spade) trophy.

Olympic gold medalist [Tom Jager](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Jager), an advocate of [high-altitude training](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-altitude_training) for swimming, has conducted training camps in Albuquerque at 5,312 feet (1,619 m) and [Los Alamos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Alamos,_New_Mexico) at 7,320 feet (2,231 m).[^cite_note-428]

New Mexico is a major hub for various [shooting sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shooting_sports), mainly concentrated in the [NRA Whittington Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NRA_Whittington_Center) in Raton, which is largest and most comprehensive competitive shooting range and training facility in the U.S.[^cite_note-429]

### Historic heritage

Owing to its millennia of habitation and over two centuries of Spanish colonial rule, New Mexico has many sites of historical and cultural significance. Forty-six locations are listed on the [U.S. National Register of Historic Places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._National_Historic_Landmark), the 18th-highest of any state.[^cite_note-430]

New Mexico has nine of the country's 84 national monuments, which are sites federally protected by presidential proclamation; this is the second-highest number after Arizona.[^cite_note-Mazurek-2021-98] The monuments include some of the earliest to have been created: [El Morro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Morro_National_Monument) and [Gila Cliff Dwellings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gila_Cliff_Dwellings_National_Monument), proclaimed in 1906 and 1907, respectively; both preserve the state's ancient indigenous heritage.[^cite_note-Mazurek-2021-98]

New Mexico is one of 20 states with a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_the_United_States), and among only eight with more than one. Excluding sites shared between states, New Mexico has the most World Heritage Sites in the United States, with three exclusively within its territory.[^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434]

Since 1970, *[New Mexico Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Magazine)* has had a standing feature, *One of Our 50 Is Missing*,[^cite_note-435] which relates anecdotes about instances in which people elsewhere do not realize New Mexico is a state, confuse it with the nation of Mexico, or otherwise mistake it for a foreign country. The state's license plates say "New Mexico USA" so as to avoid confusion with Mexico. New Mexico is the only state that specifies "USA" on its license plates.[^cite_note-436]

[^cite_note-40]: &#x20; U.S. Civil War Main articles: [New Mexico Territory in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Territory_in_the_American_Civil_War) and [New Mexico Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Campaign) Civil war effects in New MexicoNew Mexico territory including Arizona, 1860Territories divided, 1867 When the [U.S. Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) broke out in 1861, both [Confederate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Confederate_States_of_America) and [Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_\(American_Civil_War\)) governments claimed ownership and territorial rights over New Mexico Territory. The Confederacy claimed the southern tract as its own [Arizona Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_Territory_\(Confederate_States_of_America\)) and, as part of the [Trans-Mississippi Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Mississippi_Theater_of_the_American_Civil_War) of the war, waged the ambitious [New Mexico Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_Campaign) to control the [American Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Southwest) and open up access to Union California. The Confederates initially managed to conquer [Santa Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico), making it the only U.S. capital to fall to the Confederacy during the war. Confederate power in the New Mexico Territory was effectively broken after the [Battle of Glorieta Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Glorieta_Pass) in 1862, though the Confederate territorial government continued to operate out of Texas. More than 8,000 soldiers from New Mexico Territory served in the [Union Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Army).["American Civil War Research Database statistics"](https://web.archive.org/web/20120617052546/http://www.civilwardata.com/dbstatus.html). Civilwardata.com. March 4, 2012. Archived from [the original](http://www.civilwardata.com/dbstatus.html) on June 17, 2012. Retrieved June 10, 2012.
